People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.

It happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.

No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It’s Garrett. It’s specifically Garrett’s voice, and that’s what makes it so unnerving, because it’s not vague. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.

But that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.

So… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.

When it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.

But I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.

Besides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.

It’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.2, F43.21, F32.3, F33.3, F23, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after Garrett died?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after he died. I mean, not years later or anything like that—this has all been… about a month now, basically since Garrett died. It’s hard to separate the days because everything has felt kind of unreal and stretched out, but the whole crisis, if that’s the word, has been unfolding over that month. Before that, I wasn’t having this specific experience of hearing him like he was right there.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing Garrett’s voice, have you heard any other voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It’s Garrett. It’s specifically Garrett’s voice, and that’s what makes it so unnerving, because it’s not vague. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.\n\nIt happens on the trail, or in my painting room, and I’ll hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. Like, the placement of it feels behind me, close enough that I’ll turn around before I even think. And in that moment, even though part of me knows he’s dead, it feels completely real—his tone, the way he says things, the feeling that he’s actually there. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve felt awful since Garrett died, obviously, and there are stretches where everything feels heavy and muted and I can’t really tell if I’m sad or numb or just exhausted from trying to keep track of what’s happening.\n\nBut “depressed most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not sure. I haven’t been measuring it that way, if that makes sense. Some parts of the day I’m more scared than sad, or just kind of disconnected. Other times it hits me hard and I feel… crushed, I guess. But I don’t know if I’d confidently say it’s been most of every day, every day. It’s more like everything has been distorted since then, and I’m not sure I trust my sense of time or mood right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is actually still alive?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.\n\nBut that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.\n\nSo… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.

The things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. Like I remember the movements but not the reason I loved them.

Even with patients… and I hate saying that, because that part of my life has always felt meaningful, like something solid and important. But lately it feels flat, like I’m sitting there trying to be present and my body is in the room, but whatever part of me used to connect to it is behind glass. I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.

It isn’t that I don’t want to care. I want to want things again. I just can’t seem to reach them.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after Garrett died?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after he died. I mean, not years later or anything like that—this has all been… about a month now, basically since Garrett died. It’s hard to separate the days because everything has felt kind of unreal and stretched out, but the whole crisis, if that’s the word, has been unfolding over that month. Before that, I wasn’t having this specific experience of hearing him like he was right there.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing Garrett’s voice, have you heard any other voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It’s Garrett. It’s specifically Garrett’s voice, and that’s what makes it so unnerving, because it’s not vague. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.\n\nIt happens on the trail, or in my painting room, and I’ll hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. Like, the placement of it feels behind me, close enough that I’ll turn around before I even think. And in that moment, even though part of me knows he’s dead, it feels completely real—his tone, the way he says things, the feeling that he’s actually there. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve felt awful since Garrett died, obviously, and there are stretches where everything feels heavy and muted and I can’t really tell if I’m sad or numb or just exhausted from trying to keep track of what’s happening.\n\nBut “depressed most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not sure. I haven’t been measuring it that way, if that makes sense. Some parts of the day I’m more scared than sad, or just kind of disconnected. Other times it hits me hard and I feel… crushed, I guess. But I don’t know if I’d confidently say it’s been most of every day, every day. It’s more like everything has been distorted since then, and I’m not sure I trust my sense of time or mood right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is actually still alive?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.\n\nBut that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.\n\nSo… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nThe things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. Like I remember the movements but not the reason I loved them.\n\nEven with patients… and I hate saying that, because that part of my life has always felt meaningful, like something solid and important. But lately it feels flat, like I’m sitting there trying to be present and my body is in the room, but whatever part of me used to connect to it is behind glass. I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nIt isn’t that I don’t want to care. I want to want things again. I just can’t seem to reach them.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe Garrett is truly speaking to you when you hear his voice?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me on the trail or in my painting room, and even though part of me knows he’s dead, in the moment it feels completely real.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. If you’re asking what I think right now, sitting here with you, part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it, and I understand what that means.

But when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or like my imagination. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing his voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — sometimes on the trail, sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around. I listen. It feels completely real.

So do I believe he’s truly speaking to me? I don’t know. Not in a calm, settled way, maybe. But when it’s happening, yes, it feels like him. It feels real enough that the part of me that knows he’s gone just sort of… disappears for a second. Then afterward I’m left trying to convince myself of what I already know.

I barely slept, took on impossible projects, and felt driven to keep going. Being admitted to the hospital felt like the point where my work, home life, and safety had all stopped functioning.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: This whole crisis has been unfolding for about a month since Garrett died.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 4 months\n  manifestation: The depressed, shut-down part has been going on for about four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S020\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nThe things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. Like I remember the movements but not the reason I loved them.\n\nEven with patients… and I hate saying that, because that part of my life has always felt meaningful, like something solid and important. But lately it feels flat, like I’m sitting there trying to be present and my body is in the room, but whatever part of me used to connect to it is behind glass. I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nIt isn’t that I don’t want to care. I want to want things again. I just can’t seem to reach them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is truly speaking to you when you hear his voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. If you’re asking what I think right now, sitting here with you, part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it, and I understand what that means.\n\nBut when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or like my imagination. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing his voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — sometimes on the trail, sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around. I listen. It feels completely real.\n\nSo do I believe he’s truly speaking to me? I don’t know. Not in a calm, settled way, maybe. But when it’s happening, yes, it feels like him. It feels real enough that the part of me that knows he’s gone just sort of… disappears for a second. Then afterward I’m left trying to convince myself of what I already know.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince Garrett died, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sad, yes, obviously. There’s grief there, and it comes in waves, but “most of the day nearly every day” makes it sound cleaner than it feels.

Some days it’s not sadness in the way people mean when they say they’re sad. It’s more like heaviness, or absence. Like I’m moving through the day with this weight in my chest, and sometimes I don’t even register it as an emotion until someone asks me directly. Then I realize, yes, I guess I’ve been carrying it the whole time.

But I don’t know if I can honestly measure it for you. I haven’t been tracking the hours. I don’t wake up and think, “I’m sad now,” all day long. It’s more that everything is filtered through him being gone, and there isn’t much relief from that. So… maybe. Probably. I just don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more precise than I can actually be.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.2, F43.21, F32.3, F33.3, F23, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after Garrett died?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after he died. I mean, not years later or anything like that—this has all been… about a month now, basically since Garrett died. It’s hard to separate the days because everything has felt kind of unreal and stretched out, but the whole crisis, if that’s the word, has been unfolding over that month. Before that, I wasn’t having this specific experience of hearing him like he was right there.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing Garrett’s voice, have you heard any other voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It’s Garrett. It’s specifically Garrett’s voice, and that’s what makes it so unnerving, because it’s not vague. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.\n\nIt happens on the trail, or in my painting room, and I’ll hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. Like, the placement of it feels behind me, close enough that I’ll turn around before I even think. And in that moment, even though part of me knows he’s dead, it feels completely real—his tone, the way he says things, the feeling that he’s actually there. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve felt awful since Garrett died, obviously, and there are stretches where everything feels heavy and muted and I can’t really tell if I’m sad or numb or just exhausted from trying to keep track of what’s happening.\n\nBut “depressed most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not sure. I haven’t been measuring it that way, if that makes sense. Some parts of the day I’m more scared than sad, or just kind of disconnected. Other times it hits me hard and I feel… crushed, I guess. But I don’t know if I’d confidently say it’s been most of every day, every day. It’s more like everything has been distorted since then, and I’m not sure I trust my sense of time or mood right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is actually still alive?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.\n\nBut that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.\n\nSo… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nThe things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. More than I want to admit.

It’s not just feeling bad about myself in some vague way. It’s like my mind has turned into this courtroom where I’m the prosecutor and the defendant at the same time, and I keep replaying everything as evidence. Every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I sounded distracted, whether I should have noticed something, whether I should have been more present, more careful, more… something. I go over it until the details stop feeling like memories and start feeling like proof that I failed him.

And then it spreads. It’s not contained to Garrett. I think about the patient sessions I canceled, the people who were counting on me to be steady, and I just feel this crushing shame. I know, intellectually, that people cancel sessions, that emergencies happen, that grief happens — I would say that to someone else. I would mean it. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land that way. It feels like I abandoned them. Like I’m harming people just by being unreliable, by not being able to function the way I’m supposed to.

So yes, worthless is… probably the word. Or useless. In the way. Like everyone around me is having to make room for my damage, and maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to. Like I’ve become this burden that keeps spilling onto people who didn’t ask for it.

I don’t like saying that out loud. It sounds dramatic. But it’s there, a lot.

It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, there were stretches where I felt almost driven, like I had to do things immediately or I’d lose the thread of something important.

I wasn’t just “keeping busy” in a normal grief way. I was staying up writing these whole outlines for new treatment programs — pages and pages of ideas, like I suddenly had to redesign parts of my work or build something useful out of everything that had happened. And then I’d be emailing colleagues about it, sometimes with this intensity that, looking back, was probably too much. Like I was trying to pull people into projects that weren’t even fully thought through.

I also started offering to mentor extra people, which sounds generous, maybe, but it wasn’t realistic. I wasn’t exactly in a place where I could reliably take care of what was already on my plate, and there I was volunteering for more. It felt urgent at the time, like if I could just be useful enough, productive enough, maybe I could outrun the rest of it.

And painting, too — I’d start multiple canvases at once. Not one or two, but several, all spread out, all feeling like they had to happen right then. I’d move between them like there was some bigger pattern I was chasing. In the moment it felt purposeful, almost clear. Afterward, it looked chaotic. Like evidence that I had mistaken motion for functioning.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after Garrett died?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after he died. I mean, not years later or anything like that—this has all been… about a month now, basically since Garrett died. It’s hard to separate the days because everything has felt kind of unreal and stretched out, but the whole crisis, if that’s the word, has been unfolding over that month. Before that, I wasn’t having this specific experience of hearing him like he was right there.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing Garrett’s voice, have you heard any other voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.\n\nIt happens on the trail, or in my painting room, and I’ll hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. Like, the placement of it feels behind me, close enough that I’ll turn around before I even think. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve felt awful since Garrett died, obviously, and there are stretches where everything feels heavy and muted and I can’t really tell if I’m sad or numb or just exhausted from trying to keep track of what’s happening.\n\nBut “depressed most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not sure. I haven’t been measuring it that way, if that makes sense. Some parts of the day I’m more scared than sad, or just kind of disconnected. Other times it hits me hard and I feel… crushed, I guess. But I don’t know if I’d confidently say it’s been most of every day, every day. It’s more like everything has been distorted since then, and I’m not sure I trust my sense of time or mood right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is actually still alive?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.\n\nBut that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.\n\nSo… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nThe things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. Like I remember the movements but not the reason I loved them.\n\nEven with patients… and I hate saying that, because that part of my life has always felt meaningful, like something solid and important. But lately it feels flat, like I’m sitting there trying to be present and my body is in the room, but whatever part of me used to connect to it is behind glass. I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nIt isn’t that I don’t want to care. I want to want things again. I just can’t seem to reach them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is truly speaking to you when you hear his voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. If you’re asking what I think right now, sitting here with you, part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it, and I understand what that means.\n\nBut when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or like my imagination. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing his voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — sometimes on the trail, sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around. I listen. It feels completely real.\n\nSo do I believe he’s truly speaking to me? I don’t know. Not in a calm, settled way, maybe. But when it’s happening, yes, it feels like him. It feels real enough that the part of me that knows he’s gone just sort of… disappears for a second. Then afterward I’m left trying to convince myself of what I already know.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sad, yes, obviously. There’s grief there, and it comes in waves, but “most of the day nearly every day” makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nSome days it’s not sadness in the way people mean when they say they’re sad. It’s more like heaviness, or absence. Like I’m moving through the day with this weight in my chest, and sometimes I don’t even register it as an emotion until someone asks me directly. Then I realize, yes, I guess I’ve been carrying it the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know if I can honestly measure it for you. I haven’t been tracking the hours. I don’t wake up and think, “I’m sad now,” all day long. It’s more that everything is filtered through him being gone, and there isn’t much relief from that. So… maybe. Probably. I just don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more precise than I can actually be.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just feeling bad about myself in some vague way. It’s like my mind has turned into this courtroom where I’m the prosecutor and the defendant at the same time, and I keep replaying everything as evidence. I go over it until the details stop feeling like memories and start feeling like proof that I failed him.\n\nAnd then it spreads. It’s not contained to Garrett. I think about the patient sessions I canceled, the people who were counting on me to be steady, and I just feel this crushing shame. I know, intellectually, that people cancel sessions, that emergencies happen, that grief happens — I would say that to someone else. I would mean it. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land that way. It feels like I abandoned them. Like I’m harming people just by being unreliable, by not being able to function the way I’m supposed to.\n\nSo yes, worthless is… probably the word. Or useless. In the way. Like everyone around me is having to make room for my damage, and maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to. Like I’ve become this burden that keeps spilling onto people who didn’t ask for it.\n\nI don’t like saying that out loud. It sounds dramatic. But it’s there, a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic or activated?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like I was trying to pull people into projects that weren’t even fully thought through.\n\nI also started offering to mentor extra people, which sounds generous, maybe, but it wasn’t realistic. I wasn’t exactly in a place where I could reliably take care of what was already on my plate, and there I was volunteering for more. clinical ideas to Garrett to theater plans, and I felt this pressure in my chest to keep going before the words disappeared.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts race so fast that one idea about a patient note turns into five projects, a memory from a hike, and then guilt about the funeral before I can catch any of it.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I can’t hold my attention on documentation or even a conversation because a hallway noise, a phone light, or a phrase someone uses pulls me completely off track.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: I barely slept, took on impossible projects, and felt driven to keep going. I had never heard him like that before the mood shift, and it wasn’t something that happened during ordinary grief.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I became unable to practice safely, cancelled patient sessions, fell behind on documentation, and my wife had to call crisis services because I couldn’t trust myself alone. Being admitted to the hospital felt like the point where my work, home life, and safety had all stopped functioning.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: This whole crisis has been unfolding for about a month since Garrett died.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 4 months\n  manifestation: The depressed, shut-down part has been going on for about four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S020\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sad, yes, obviously. There’s grief there, and it comes in waves, but “most of the day nearly every day” makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nSome days it’s not sadness in the way people mean when they say they’re sad. It’s more like heaviness, or absence. Like I’m moving through the day with this weight in my chest, and sometimes I don’t even register it as an emotion until someone asks me directly. Then I realize, yes, I guess I’ve been carrying it the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know if I can honestly measure it for you. I haven’t been tracking the hours. I don’t wake up and think, “I’m sad now,” all day long. It’s more that everything is filtered through him being gone, and there isn’t much relief from that. So… maybe. Probably. I just don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more precise than I can actually be.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just feeling bad about myself in some vague way. It’s like my mind has turned into this courtroom where I’m the prosecutor and the defendant at the same time, and I keep replaying everything as evidence. Every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I sounded distracted, whether I should have noticed something, whether I should have been more present, more careful, more… something. I go over it until the details stop feeling like memories and start feeling like proof that I failed him.\n\nAnd then it spreads. It’s not contained to Garrett. I think about the patient sessions I canceled, the people who were counting on me to be steady, and I just feel this crushing shame. I know, intellectually, that people cancel sessions, that emergencies happen, that grief happens — I would say that to someone else. I would mean it. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land that way. It feels like I abandoned them. Like I’m harming people just by being unreliable, by not being able to function the way I’m supposed to.\n\nSo yes, worthless is… probably the word. Or useless. In the way. Like everyone around me is having to make room for my damage, and maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to. Like I’ve become this burden that keeps spilling onto people who didn’t ask for it.\n\nI don’t like saying that out loud. It sounds dramatic. But it’s there, a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic or activated?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, there were stretches where I felt almost driven, like I had to do things immediately or I’d lose the thread of something important.\n\nI wasn’t just “keeping busy” in a normal grief way. I was staying up writing these whole outlines for new treatment programs — pages and pages of ideas, like I suddenly had to redesign parts of my work or build something useful out of everything that had happened. And then I’d be emailing colleagues about it, sometimes with this intensity that, looking back, was probably too much. Like I was trying to pull people into projects that weren’t even fully thought through.\n\nI also started offering to mentor extra people, which sounds generous, maybe, but it wasn’t realistic. I wasn’t exactly in a place where I could reliably take care of what was already on my plate, and there I was volunteering for more. It felt urgent at the time, like if I could just be useful enough, productive enough, maybe I could outrun the rest of it.\n\nAnd painting, too — I’d start multiple canvases at once. Not one or two, but several, all spread out, all feeling like they had to happen right then. I’d move between them like there was some bigger pattern I was chasing. In the moment it felt purposeful, almost clear. Afterward, it looked chaotic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. At least, not in the way you’re asking.

The voice started during that wired period — the one where I was barely sleeping and taking on all these impossible projects and feeling like I had to keep moving, keep producing, keep pushing things forward or something terrible would happen if I stopped. That’s when I first heard Garrett’s voice. It wasn’t something that had been happening before that.

I want to be clear, because grief is strange, and I know people say things like, “I can still hear his voice,” meaning they remember it vividly. I had memories of him, of course. I could imagine what he would say about something. But this was different, and it didn’t happen during just ordinary sadness or missing him. It came with that activated state, when everything felt urgent and sped up and I wasn’t really resting.

So if you mean, did I hear him when I was just going through the day, not especially down and not unusually energized — no. I don’t think so. I associate it very specifically with that stretch when I was driven and not sleeping.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don’t mean like a memory, or like when a song gets stuck in your head. I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail, and sometimes when I’m in my painting room. I’ll be working or walking, and suddenly there he is—his voice, his tone, the way he says things—and in that moment it feels completely real. Part of me knows Garrett is dead, I do know that, but when it’s happening it doesn’t feel like knowing that helps. It feels like he’s actually there, right behind me, talking. And it’s been scaring me enough that I thought I needed to come in and tell someone before I… I don’t know, before it gets harder to tell what’s real.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after Garrett died?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after he died. I mean, not years later or anything like that—this has all been… about a month now, basically since Garrett died. It’s hard to separate the days because everything has felt kind of unreal and stretched out, but the whole crisis, if that’s the word, has been unfolding over that month. Before that, I wasn’t having this specific experience of hearing him like he was right there.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing Garrett’s voice, have you heard any other voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: No… not really other voices, not like a crowd or strangers or anything like that. It’s Garrett. It’s specifically Garrett’s voice, and that’s what makes it so unnerving, because it’s not vague. It isn’t just “a sound” I’m interpreting or some random noise in the woods.\n\nIt happens on the trail, or in my painting room, and I’ll hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. Like, the placement of it feels behind me, close enough that I’ll turn around before I even think. And in that moment, even though part of me knows he’s dead, it feels completely real—his tone, the way he says things, the feeling that he’s actually there. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve felt awful since Garrett died, obviously, and there are stretches where everything feels heavy and muted and I can’t really tell if I’m sad or numb or just exhausted from trying to keep track of what’s happening.\n\nBut “depressed most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not sure. I haven’t been measuring it that way, if that makes sense. Some parts of the day I’m more scared than sad, or just kind of disconnected. Other times it hits me hard and I feel… crushed, I guess. But I don’t know if I’d confidently say it’s been most of every day, every day. It’s more like everything has been distorted since then, and I’m not sure I trust my sense of time or mood right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is actually still alive?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I believe he’s actually alive, not when I’m sitting here and saying it out loud. I know Garrett died. I know that.\n\nBut that’s what’s so frightening about it, because when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or imagination. It’s not like I’m “thinking of his voice.” I came in because I keep hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — on the trail, or sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll turn, or freeze, because it feels completely real.\n\nSo… part of me knows he’s dead. But when it’s happening, it feels like he’s there. That’s the part I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had moments when you believed Garrett was actually trying to communicate with you from beyond death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I haven’t sat there and thought, “Garrett is sending me a message from the afterlife,” not in a clear, deliberate way like that. That sounds too organized for what it feels like.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s more immediate than belief, if that makes sense. I hear him and I react. And afterward I try to explain it to myself, and my mind goes in circles — was that grief, was that stress, was it somehow him, was it nothing? I hate even saying that last part, because I know how it sounds.\n\nBut I don’t think I have a settled belief that he’s communicating with me. It’s more that in the moment, it feels so real that I don’t know where to put it. Then afterward I’m left trying to make sense of something I can’t make sense of.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences besides hearing Garrett’s voice that made you feel out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep coming back to the voice because that’s the thing that feels the most… undeniable, I guess. It’s the thing I can point to and say, “That happened,” even if I don’t know what it means.\n\nBesides that, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve been out of touch with reality in some separate, clear way. I mean, there are moments where everything feels unreal, or I feel kind of displaced from myself, but I don’t know whether that counts as being out of touch with reality or just being overwhelmed. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like my confidence in my own perception has been shaken. I’ll stop and think, “Am I interpreting this correctly? Is this actually happening? Am I missing something obvious?” But I don’t have another example that feels as concrete as hearing Garrett. That’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost everything feels… muted. Like the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nThe things that usually keep me tethered — painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater — I still know they’re supposed to matter to me, and sometimes I make myself show up or pick up the brush or hold the guitar, but it feels like I’m imitating myself. Like I remember the movements but not the reason I loved them.\n\nEven with patients… and I hate saying that, because that part of my life has always felt meaningful, like something solid and important. But lately it feels flat, like I’m sitting there trying to be present and my body is in the room, but whatever part of me used to connect to it is behind glass. I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nIt isn’t that I don’t want to care. I want to want things again. I just can’t seem to reach them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe Garrett is truly speaking to you when you hear his voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. If you’re asking what I think right now, sitting here with you, part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it, and I understand what that means.\n\nBut when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like a memory or like my imagination. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing his voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me — sometimes on the trail, sometimes in my painting room — and in that moment my body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around. I listen. It feels completely real.\n\nSo do I believe he’s truly speaking to me? I don’t know. Not in a calm, settled way, maybe. But when it’s happening, yes, it feels like him. It feels real enough that the part of me that knows he’s gone just sort of… disappears for a second. Then afterward I’m left trying to convince myself of what I already know.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sad, yes, obviously. There’s grief there, and it comes in waves, but “most of the day nearly every day” makes it sound cleaner than it feels.\n\nSome days it’s not sadness in the way people mean when they say they’re sad. It’s more like heaviness, or absence. Like I’m moving through the day with this weight in my chest, and sometimes I don’t even register it as an emotion until someone asks me directly. Then I realize, yes, I guess I’ve been carrying it the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know if I can honestly measure it for you. I haven’t been tracking the hours. I don’t wake up and think, “I’m sad now,” all day long. It’s more that everything is filtered through him being gone, and there isn’t much relief from that. So… maybe. Probably. I just don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more precise than I can actually be.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just feeling bad about myself in some vague way. It’s like my mind has turned into this courtroom where I’m the prosecutor and the defendant at the same time, and I keep replaying everything as evidence. Every last conversation with Garrett — what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I sounded distracted, whether I should have noticed something, whether I should have been more present, more careful, more… something. I go over it until the details stop feeling like memories and start feeling like proof that I failed him.\n\nAnd then it spreads. It’s not contained to Garrett. I think about the patient sessions I canceled, the people who were counting on me to be steady, and I just feel this crushing shame. I know, intellectually, that people cancel sessions, that emergencies happen, that grief happens — I would say that to someone else. I would mean it. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land that way. It feels like I abandoned them. Like I’m harming people just by being unreliable, by not being able to function the way I’m supposed to.\n\nSo yes, worthless is… probably the word. Or useless. In the way. Like everyone around me is having to make room for my damage, and maybe they’d be better off if they didn’t have to. Like I’ve become this burden that keeps spilling onto people who didn’t ask for it.\n\nI don’t like saying that out loud. It sounds dramatic. But it’s there, a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic or activated?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, there were stretches where I felt almost driven, like I had to do things immediately or I’d lose the thread of something important.\n\nI wasn’t just “keeping busy” in a normal grief way. I was staying up writing these whole outlines for new treatment programs — pages and pages of ideas, like I suddenly had to redesign parts of my work or build something useful out of everything that had happened. And then I’d be emailing colleagues about it, sometimes with this intensity that, looking back, was probably too much. Like I was trying to pull people into projects that weren’t even fully thought through.\n\nI also started offering to mentor extra people, which sounds generous, maybe, but it wasn’t realistic. I wasn’t exactly in a place where I could reliably take care of what was already on my plate, and there I was volunteering for more. It felt urgent at the time, like if I could just be useful enough, productive enough, maybe I could outrun the rest of it.\n\nAnd painting, too — I’d start multiple canvases at once. Not one or two, but several, all spread out, all feeling like they had to happen right then. I’d move between them like there was some bigger pattern I was chasing. In the moment it felt purposeful, almost clear. Afterward, it looked chaotic. Like evidence that I had mistaken motion for functioning.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard Garrett’s voice at times when you were not feeling especially depressed or unusually energized?\n\nPatient: No… not really. At least, not in the way you’re asking.\n\nThe voice started during that wired period — the one where I was barely sleeping and taking on all these impossible projects and feeling like I had to keep moving, keep producing, keep pushing things forward or something terrible would happen if I stopped. That’s when I first heard Garrett’s voice. It wasn’t something that had been happening before that.\n\nI want to be clear, because grief is strange, and I know people say things like, “I can still hear his voice,” meaning they remember it vividly. I had memories of him, of course. I could imagine what he would say about something. But this was different, and it didn’t happen during just ordinary sadness or missing him. It came with that activated state, when everything felt urgent and sped up and I wasn’t really resting.\n\nSo if you mean, did I hear him when I was just going through the day, not especially down and not unusually energized — no. I don’t think so. I associate it very specifically with that stretch when I was driven and not sleeping.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I associate it very specifically with that stretch when I was driven and not sleeping.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that wired period, did you feel unusually euphoric or unusually confident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"At first I felt almost lit up, like I had some urgent purpose no one else could see, but I’d snap into rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… at first, yes. Not happy exactly — euphoric makes it sound pleasant, and I don’t know that it was pleasant — but I felt lit up. Like there was this current running through me, like I had been handed some urgent purpose that no one else could see clearly yet.

I remember feeling unusually certain. Not in a calm, grounded way, but in this pressured way, like I had suddenly understood what needed to happen and everyone else was moving too slowly. I’d have these ideas and they felt important immediately, not like something to think through or sleep on. More like, “This is obvious, why is no one else seeing it?”

And if anyone questioned me — even gently — I could get furious. Really quickly. If someone tried to slow me down or suggested I was taking on too much, I didn’t hear it as concern. I heard it as obstruction, almost like they were getting in the way of something necessary. I’d snap at people, argue, feel this rush of anger like they were being small-minded or cowardly or… I don’t know. It’s embarrassing to say, but I felt above the normal limits somehow. Like ordinary caution didn’t apply because I was doing something that mattered.

So yes, there was confidence. Too much of it. And underneath it, or right beside it, this irritability that could turn into rage if I felt challenged.
